Hilbert expansion for kinetic equations with non-relativistic Coulomb collision Thanks: Acknowledgment. The research of Yuanjie Lei was supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China grants 11871335 and 11971187, the research of Shuangqian Liu was supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China grant 11971201, the research of Qinghua Xiao was supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China grants 11871469 and 12271506, the research of Huijiang Zhao was supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China grants 11731008 and 12221001. This work was also partially supported by the Fundamental Research Funds for the Central Universities and by the Science and Technology Department of Hubei Province grant 2020DFH002.
Yuanjie Lei, Shuangqian Liu, Qinghua Xiao, Huijiang Zhao

Abstract
. In this paper, we study the hydrodynamic limits of both the Landau equation and the Vlasov-Maxwell-Landau system in the whole space. Our main purpose is two-fold: the first one is to give a rigorous derivation of the compressible Euler equations from the Landau equation via the Hilbert expansion; while the second one is to prove, still in the setting of Hilbert expansion, that the unique classical solution of the Vlasov-Maxwell-Landau system converges, which is shown to be globally in time, to the resulting global smooth solution of the Euler-Maxwell system, as the Knudsen number goes to zero. The main ingredient of our analysis is to derive some novel interplay energy estimates on the solutions of the Landau equation and the Vlasov-Maxwell-Landau system which are small perturbations of both a local Maxwellian and a global Maxwellian, respectively. Our result solves an open problem in the hydrodynamic limit for the Landau-type equations with Coulomb potential and the approach developed in this paper can seamlessly be used to deal with the problem on the validity of the Hilbert expansion for other types of kinetic equations.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
76Y05; 35Q20

1.
Introduction

The continuous transitions from the kinetic equations for perfect gases or particles to hydrodynamics is related to the sixth problem of Hilbert. As the first example of the program proposed by himself, Hilbert introduced the famous Hilbert expansion in the Knudsen number
\varepsilon
cf.
[
30
,
54
]
. The rigorous justification of the validity of such an expansion for kinetic equations is a challenging problem since then.

In this article, at the kinetic level, we consider the following rescaled Landau equation

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}F^{\varepsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F^{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{C}(F^{\varepsilon},F^{\varepsilon})\end{aligned}

(1.1)

and the rescaled Vlasov-Maxwell-Landau (VML for short in the sequel) system

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}F^{\varepsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F^{\varepsilon}-e_{-}\Big(E^{\epsilon}+v\times B^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F^{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{C}(F^{\varepsilon},F^{\varepsilon}),\\
&\partial_{t}E^{\varepsilon}-c\nabla_{x}\times B^{\varepsilon}=4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}vF^{\varepsilon}dv,\\
&\partial_{t}B^{\varepsilon}+c\nabla_{x}\times E^{\varepsilon}=0,\\
&\nabla_{x}\cdot E^{\varepsilon}=4\pi e_{-}\Big(1-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}F^{\varepsilon}dv\Big),\qquad\nabla_{x}\cdot B^{\varepsilon}=0.\end{aligned}

(1.2)

Here,
\varepsilon>0
is the Knudsen number,
\nabla_{x}=(\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}},\partial_{x_{3}})
,
\nabla_{v}=(\partial_{v_{1}},\partial_{v_{2}},\partial_{v_{3}})
. The unknown function
F^{\varepsilon}=F^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)\geq 0
is the number density function for electrons at time
t\geq 0
, position
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and velocity
v=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
-e_{-}
is the charge of the electrons,
c
is the speed of light, and
[E^{\varepsilon},B^{\varepsilon}]=[E^{\varepsilon}(t,x),B^{\varepsilon}(t,x)]
stands for the electromagnetic field. Without loss of generality and for notational simplicity, we normalize the constants
e_{-}
and
c
to be one in the rest of this paper. Moreover, the Landau collision operator
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\cdot)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(G,H)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{v}\cdot\left\{\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\phi(v-v^{\prime})\left[G(v^{\prime})\nabla_{v}H(v)-H(v)\nabla_{v^{\prime}}G(v^{\prime})\right]dv^{\prime}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{3}\partial_{v_{i}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\phi^{ij}(v-v^{\prime})\left[G(v^{\prime})\partial_{v_{j}}H(v)-H(v)\partial_{v^{\prime}_{j}}G(v^{\prime})\right]dv^{\prime}.

\phi(v):=\left(\phi^{ij}(v)\right)_{3\times 3}
is a non-negative matrix with
\phi^{ij}(v)
being given by

\phi^{ij}(v)=\left\{\delta_{ij}-\frac{v_{i}v_{j}}{|v|^{2}}\right\}|v|^{\gamma+2}.

(1.3)

Here,
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta and
\gamma
is a parameter leading to the standard classification of the hard potential
(\gamma>0)
, Maxwellian molecule
(\gamma=0)
or soft potential
(\gamma<0)
. In this paper, we focus on the case of
\gamma=-3
, which correspondes to the Coulomb interaction in plasma physics.

The purpose of this paper is to rigorously justify that the Hilbert expansion is valid for both the Landau equation (
1.1
) and the VML system (
1.2
) in the whole space with the initial datum

\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon}(0,x,v)=F_{0}^{\varepsilon}(x,v)

(1.4)

for the Landau equation (
1.1
) and

\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon}(0,x,v)=F_{0}^{\varepsilon}(x,v),\ E^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=E^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x),\ B^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=B^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)

(1.5)

for the VML system (
1.2
), respectively.

To make the presentation clear, we divide the rest of this section into several subsections and the first one is concerned with the formal Hilbert expansions for the Landau equation (
1.1
) and the VML system (
1.2
).

1.1.
Hilbert expansions

This subsection focuses on the formal Hilbert expansion for both the Landau equation (
1.1
) and the VML system (
1.2
).

1.1.1.
Hilbert expansion of the Landau equation

Let
k\geq 2
be some integer, we consider the following Hilbert expansion for the Landau equation (
1.1
)

\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)=\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}F_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}F^{\varepsilon}_{R}.

(1.6)

To determine the coefficients
F_{0}(t,x,v),\ldots,F_{2k-1}(t,x,v)
, we plug the above expansion into the rescaled equations (
1.1
) to obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}F_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}F^{\varepsilon}_{R}\right)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}F_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}F^{\varepsilon}_{R}\right)

(1.7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{C}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}F_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}F^{\varepsilon}_{R},\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}F_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}F^{\varepsilon}_{R}\right),

and from which one can deduce by comparing the coefficients of
\varepsilon^{k}
for
k=-1,0,\cdots,2k-1
on both side of the above equation that the coefficients
F_{j}(t,x,v)(j=0,1,\cdots,2k-1)
satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}:

\displaystyle\quad~\mathcal{C}(F_{0},F_{0})=0,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{0}:

\displaystyle\quad~\partial_{t}F_{0}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{0}=\mathcal{C}(F_{1},F_{0})+\mathcal{C}(F_{0},F_{1}),

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots

(1.8)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{n}:

\displaystyle\quad~\partial_{t}F_{n}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{n}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=n+1\\
i,j\geq 0\end{subarray}}\mathcal{C}(F_{i},F_{j}),

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2k-1}:

\displaystyle\quad~\partial_{t}F_{2k-1}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{2k-1}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=2k\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\mathcal{C}(F_{i},F_{j}).

Then we see from (
1.7
) and (
1.8
) that the remainder term
F_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left[\mathcal{C}\left(F_{R}^{\varepsilon},F_{0}\right)+\mathcal{C}\left(F_{0},F_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{k-1}\mathcal{C}\left(F_{R}^{\varepsilon},F_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i-1}\left[\mathcal{C}\left(F_{i}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right)+\mathcal{C}\left(F_{R}^{\varepsilon},F_{i}\right)\right]+\mathcal{P},

with
\mathcal{P}=\sum\limits_{i+j\geq 2k+1\atop{2\leq i,j\leq 2k-1}}\varepsilon^{i+j-k}\mathcal{C}(F_{i},F_{j})
.

(
1.8
)
1
tells us that
F_{0}(t,x,v)
is nothing but a local Maxwellian
\mathbf{M}
, i.e.

F_{0}(t,x,v)=\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}_{[\rho,u,T]}=\frac{\rho(t,x)}{(2\pi RT(t,x))^{3/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|v-u(t,x)|^{2}}{2RT(t,x)}\right).

(1.9)

Here
\rho(t,x),u(t,x)
and
T(t,x)
are the fluid density, bulk velocity and temperature, respectively,
R
is the gas constant which will be taken to be
\frac{2}{3}
in this paper for convenience.

With this local Maxwellian
\mathbf{M}
, we write
F^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)
as

F^{\varepsilon}=\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{\varepsilon},

(1.10)

then it is easy to see that
f^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)
satisfies

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\big)f^{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}[f^{\varepsilon}]=

\displaystyle-f^{\varepsilon}\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\big)\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\varepsilon^{k-1}\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f^{\varepsilon},f^{\varepsilon})

(1.11)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i-1}\Big[\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{i},f^{\varepsilon})+\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f^{\varepsilon},\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{i})\Big]+\mathcal{P}_{0},

where
\mathcal{P}_{0}=\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}[f]
is the linearized Landau collision operator defined by

\displaystyle-\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}[f]=\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}[f]+\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}[f]=

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big[\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f)+\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f,\mathbf{M})\big],

(1.12)

and

\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f,g)
is the nonlinear Landau collision operator given by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f,g)=

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f,\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}g).

(1.13)

Moreover,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}[f]
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}[f]
are defined as

\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}[f]=\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f),\qquad\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}[f]=\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f,\mathbf{M}).

On the other hand, we also introduce a global Maxwellian

\mu=\frac{1}{(2\pi RT_{c})^{3/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|v|^{2}}{2RT_{c}}\right),

(1.14)

where
T_{c}>0
is a positive constant whose precise range will be specified in Corollary
1.1
, and set

F_{R}^{\varepsilon}=\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h^{\varepsilon}.

(1.15)

Then it is straightforward to see that
h^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)
satisfies the following equation

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\big)h^{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{L}[h^{\varepsilon}]=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{L}_{d}[h^{\varepsilon}]+\varepsilon^{k-1}\Gamma(h^{\varepsilon},h^{\varepsilon})

(1.16)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i-1}\Gamma(\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{i},h^{\varepsilon})+\mathcal{C}(h^{\varepsilon},\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{i})]+\mathcal{P}_{1},

where
\mathcal{P}_{1}=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{L}[f]
and
\Gamma(f,g)
are given by

\displaystyle-\mathcal{L}[f]=\mathcal{A}[f]+\mathcal{K}[f]=

\displaystyle\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left[\mathcal{C}(\mu,\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}f)+\mathcal{C}(\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}f,\mu)\right]

(1.17)

and

\displaystyle\Gamma(f,g)=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{C}(\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}f,\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}g),

respectively.

In addition,
\mathcal{L}_{d}[f]
is the difference of the linear collision operators
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}[f]
and
\mathcal{L}[f]
:

-\mathcal{L}_{d}[f]=\mathcal{A}_{d}[f]+\mathcal{K}_{d}[f]=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left[\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{M}-\mu,\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}f)+\mathcal{C}(\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}f,\mathbf{M}-\mu)\right].

(1.18)

Note that the explicit expressions of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}
,
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{d}
and
\mathcal{K}_{d}
will be given in Section
2
.

1.1.2.
Hilbert expansion of the VML system

For the VML system (
1.2
) and some integer
k\geq 2
, we consider the following Hilbert expansion

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &F^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}F_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}F^{\varepsilon}_{R},\quad E^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}E_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}E^{\varepsilon}_{R},\quad B^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}B_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}B^{\varepsilon}_{R}.\end{aligned}

(1.19)

Similar to that of the Landau equation (
1.1
), we can deduce that the coefficients
[F_{n}(t,x,v),

E_{n}(t,x),B_{n}(t,x)]
with
0\leq n\leq 2k-1
and the remainder
[F_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v),E_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x),

B_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)]

solve

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}:

\displaystyle\quad\mathcal{C}(F_{0},F_{0})=0,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{0}:

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}F_{0}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{0}-\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{0}=\mathcal{C}(F_{1},F_{0})+\mathcal{C}(F_{0},F_{1}),

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}E-\nabla_{x}\times B=4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}vF_{0}dv,

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}B+\nabla_{x}\times E=0,

\displaystyle\quad\nabla_{x}\cdot E=4\pi\Big(1-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}F_{0}dv\Big),\qquad\nabla_{x}\cdot B=0,

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots

(1.20)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{n}:

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}F_{n}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{n}-\Big(E_{n}+v\times B_{n}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{p}F_{0}-\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{n}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=n+1\\
i,j\geq 0\end{subarray}}\mathcal{C}(F_{i},F_{j})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=n\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\Big(E_{i}+v\times B_{i}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{j},

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}E_{n}-\nabla_{x}\times B_{n}=4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}vF_{n}dv,

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}B_{n}+\nabla_{x}\times E_{n}=0,

\displaystyle\quad\nabla_{x}\cdot E_{n}=-4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}F_{n}dv,\qquad\nabla_{x}\cdot B_{n}=0,

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2k-1}:

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}F_{2k-1}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{2k-1}-\Big(E_{2k-1}+v\times B_{2k-1}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{0}-\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{2k-1}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=2k\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\mathcal{C}(F_{i},F_{j})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=2k-1\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\Big(E_{i}+v\times B_{i}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{j},

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}E_{2k-1}-\nabla_{x}\times B_{2k-1}=4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}vF_{2k-1}dv,

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}B_{2k-1}+\nabla_{x}\times E_{2k-1}=0,

\displaystyle\quad\nabla_{x}\cdot E_{2k-1}=-4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}F_{2k-1}dv,\qquad\nabla_{x}\cdot B_{2k-1}=0

and

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{0}

\displaystyle\qquad-\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}[\mathcal{C}(F_{R}^{\varepsilon},F_{0})+\mathcal{C}(F_{0},F_{R}^{\varepsilon})]

\displaystyle\quad=\varepsilon^{k-1}\mathcal{C}(F_{R}^{\varepsilon},F_{R}^{\varepsilon})+\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i-1}[\mathcal{C}(F_{i},F_{R}^{\varepsilon})+\mathcal{C}(F_{R}^{\varepsilon},F_{i})]+\varepsilon^{k}\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i}\Big[\Big(E_{i}+v\times B_{i}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{i}\Big]+\varepsilon^{k}\mathcal{Q},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\nabla_{x}\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}=4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}vF_{R}^{\varepsilon}dv,

(1.21)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}B_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\nabla_{x}\times E_{R}^{\varepsilon}=0,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E_{R}^{\varepsilon}=-4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}dv,\qquad\nabla_{x}\cdot B_{R}^{\varepsilon}=0,

respectively. Here we have denoted
[E_{0},B_{0}]
by
[E,B]
and

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{Q}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\geq 2k+1\\
2\leq i,j\leq 2k-1\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{i+j-2k-1}Q(F_{i},F_{j})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\geq 2k\\
1\leq i,j\leq 2k-1\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{i+j-2k}\Big(E_{i}+v\times B_{i}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{j}.\end{aligned}

(
1.20
)
1
implies that
F_{0}(t,x,v)=\mathbf{M}(t,x,v)
with
\mathbf{M}(t,x,v)
being given by (
1.9
).
If we define
f^{\varepsilon}
as in (
1.10
), then we can rewrite (
1.21
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f^{\varepsilon}+\frac{\big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\big)}{RT}\cdot\big(v-u\big)\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\frac{v-u}{2RT}f^{\varepsilon}-\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{\varepsilon}+\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}[f^{\varepsilon}]}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}f^{\varepsilon}\Big[\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}-\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}\Big]\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\varepsilon^{k-1}\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f^{\varepsilon},f^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i-1}\Big[\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{i},f^{\varepsilon})+\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f^{\varepsilon},\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{i})\Big]+\varepsilon^{k}\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle-\varepsilon^{k}\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\frac{v-u}{2RT}f^{\varepsilon}

(1.22)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i}\Big[\Big(E_{i}+v\times B_{i}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{\varepsilon}+\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{i}\Big]

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i}\Big[\Big(E_{i}+v\times B_{i}\Big)\cdot\frac{\big(v-u\big)}{2RT}f^{\varepsilon}\Big]+\varepsilon^{k}\mathcal{Q}_{0}

and

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\nabla_{x}\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}v\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{\varepsilon}dv,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}B_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\nabla_{x}\times E_{R}^{\varepsilon}=0,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E_{R}^{\varepsilon}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{\varepsilon}dv,\qquad\nabla_{x}\cdot B_{R}^{\varepsilon}=0,

where
\mathcal{Q}_{0}=\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{Q}
.

Moreover, if we define
h^{\varepsilon}
as in (
1.15
), we can then deduce that
h^{\varepsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{t}h^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+v\cdot\nabla_{x}h^{\varepsilon}+\frac{\big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\big)}{RT}\cdot\big(v-u\big)\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{M}

\displaystyle+\frac{E\cdot v}{2RT_{c}}h^{\varepsilon}-\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}h^{\varepsilon}+\frac{\mathcal{L}[h^{\varepsilon}]}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathcal{L}_{d}[h^{\varepsilon}]}{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{k-1}\Gamma(h^{\varepsilon},h^{\varepsilon})+\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i-1}[\Gamma(\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{i},h^{\varepsilon})+\Gamma(h^{\varepsilon},\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{i})]

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{k}\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}h^{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon^{k}\frac{E_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot v}{2RT_{c}}h^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i}\Big[\Big(E_{i}+v\times B_{i}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}h^{\varepsilon}+\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{i}\Big]-\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i}\frac{E_{i}\cdot v}{2RT_{c}}h^{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{k}\mathcal{Q}_{1},

where
\mathcal{Q}_{1}=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{Q}
.

Remark 1.1
.

To simplify notation, without causing confusion, for both the Landau equation (
1.1
) and the VML system (
1.2
),
we will use
F^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)
to denote their solutions,
f_{j}(t,x,v)
to stand for the coefficients of the Hilbert expansion with
0\leq j\leq 2k-1
,
f^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)
and
h^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)
the corresponding remainders. It is easy to see that
\mathbf{M}_{[\rho,u,T]}:=\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{[\rho,u,T]}f_{0}(t,x,v)
.

1.2.
Notations

Throughout this paper,
C
denotes a generic positive constant which is independent of the Knudsen number
\varepsilon
but may change line by line. The notation
a\lesssim b
implies that there exists a generic positive constant
C
independent of the Knudsen number
\varepsilon
such that
a\leq Cb
and
a\approx b
means that both
a\lesssim b
and
b\lesssim a
hold.

The multi-indices
\alpha=[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}]
and
\beta=[\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}]
will be used to record spatial and velocity derivatives, respectively. And
\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}=\partial^{\alpha_{1}}_{x_{1}}\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{x_{2}}\partial^{\alpha_{3}}_{x_{3}}\partial^{\beta_{1}}_{v_{1}}\partial^{\beta_{2}}_{v_{2}}\partial^{\beta_{3}}_{v_{3}}
. Similarly, the notation
\partial^{\alpha}
will be used when
\beta=0
and likewise for
\partial_{\beta}
. The length of
\alpha
is denoted by
|\alpha|=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}
.
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha
means that no component of
\alpha^{\prime}
is greater than the corresponding component of
\alpha
, and
\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha
means that
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha
and
|\alpha^{\prime}|<|\alpha|
. And it is convenient to write
\nabla_{x}^{k}=\partial^{\alpha}
with
|\alpha|=k
.

(\cdot,\cdot)
is used to denote the
L^{2}\times L^{2}
inner product in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}
, with the
{L^{2}}
norm
|\cdot|_{L^{2}}
. For notational simplicity,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the
{L^{2}}\times L^{2}
inner product either in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{x}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}
or in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{x}
with the
{L^{2}}\times L^{2}
norm
\|\cdot\|
. For each non-negative integer
k
and
1\leq p\leq+\infty
, we also use
W^{k,p}
to denote the standard Sobolev spaces for
(x,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
or
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, and denote
H^{s}=W^{s,2}
with

\|f\|^{2}_{H^{s}}:=\sum_{|\alpha|=0}^{s}\|\partial^{\alpha}f\|^{2}.
To simplify the presentation, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we use
\left(\nabla_{x}^{i}f,\nabla^{i}_{x}g\right)
and
\left\langle\nabla_{x}^{i}f,\nabla^{i}_{x}g\right\rangle
to denote
\sum_{|\alpha|=i}\left(\partial^{\alpha}f,\partial^{\alpha}g\right)
and
\sum_{|\alpha|=i}\left\langle\partial^{\alpha}f,\partial^{\alpha}g\right\rangle
, respectively. Recall that
\nabla_{x}=(\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}},\partial_{x_{3}})
,
\nabla_{v}=(\partial_{v_{1}},\partial_{v_{2}},\partial_{v_{3}})
.

Let

\sigma^{ij}(v)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\phi^{ij}(v-v^{\prime})\mu(v^{\prime})dv^{\prime}

with
\phi^{ij}(v)
being defined by (
1.3
), we now introduce the dissipation norm in
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
by

\displaystyle|f(t,x)|_{D}^{2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\sigma^{ij}(v)\partial_{v_{i}}f(t,x,v)\partial_{v_{j}}f(t,x,v)\,dv

(1.23)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4R^{2}T^{2}_{c}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\sigma^{ij}(v)v_{i}v_{j}|f(t,x,v)|^{2}\,dv,

the corresponding dissipation norm in
(x,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
by

\displaystyle\|f(t)\|_{D}^{2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\sigma^{ij}(v)\partial_{v_{i}}f(t,x,v)\partial_{v_{j}}f(t,x,v)\,dvdx

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4R^{2}T^{2}_{c}}\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\sigma^{ij}(v)v_{i}v_{j}|f(t,x,v)|^{2}\,dvdx,

and the Sobolev dissipation norm

\displaystyle\|f(t)\|^{2}_{H^{s}_{D}}:=\sum_{|\alpha|=0}^{s}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}f(t)\right\|^{2}_{D},\qquad s\geq 2.

Here and in the sequel, Einstein’s summation convention is used.

For constant
\ell\leq s
, we set
\langle v\rangle=\sqrt{1+|v|^{2}}
and introduce the following velocity weight functions

\displaystyle w_{i}(t,v):=\langle v\rangle^{\ell-i}\exp\left(\frac{(1+|v|^{2})}{8RT_{c}\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)}\right),\quad 0\leq i\leq s

(1.24)

and then define the weighted norms

\displaystyle\|wh\|^{2}_{H^{s}}:=\sum_{i=0}^{s}\left\|w_{i}\nabla_{x}^{i}h\right\|^{2},\qquad\|wh\|^{2}_{H^{s}_{D}}:=\sum_{i=0}^{s}\left\|w_{i}\nabla_{x}^{i}h\right\|^{2}_{D}.

For simplicity, we also denote

\displaystyle X(t):=\exp\left(\frac{1}{8RT_{c}\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)}\right)

and it is eay to see that

\displaystyle Y(t):=-\frac{X^{\prime}(t)}{X(t)}=\frac{1}{8RT_{c}(\mathrm{e}+t)\big(\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)\big)^{2}}>0.

Finally, it is easy to see that the null space of the linearized Landau operator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}
is given by

\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{M}}=\mbox{span}\left\{\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}},(v_{i}-u_{i})\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}(1\leq i\leq 3),\left(\frac{|v-u|^{2}}{RT}-3\right)\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\},

while the null space of the linearized operator
\mathcal{L}
is given by

\mathcal{N}=\mbox{span}\left\{\mu^{\frac{1}{2}},v_{i}\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(1\leq i\leq 3),(|v|^{2}-3)\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}.

We now define
{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}
as the orthogonal projection from
L^{2}_{v}
onto
\mathcal{N}_{\bf M}
,
and
{\bf P}
as the orthogonal projection from
L^{2}_{v}
onto
\mathcal{N}
, respectively. Furthermore, let us use
\chi_{j}
(0\leq j\leq 4)
to denote the orthonormal basis of the null space of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}
and be given by

\displaystyle\chi_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{\mathbf{M}},\ \chi_{i}=\frac{v_{i}-u_{i}}{{\sqrt{R\rho T}}}\sqrt{\mathbf{M}},\ \ i=1,2,3,\ \chi_{4}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{6\rho}}\left(\frac{|v-u|^{2}}{2RT}-3\right)\sqrt{\mathbf{M}},

then we write

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}[f_{n}]=\frac{\rho_{n}}{\sqrt{\rho}}\chi_{0}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{3}\frac{1}{\sqrt{R\rho T}}u_{n}^{i}\chi_{i}+\frac{T_{n}}{\sqrt{6\rho}}\chi_{4}.

(1.25)

Here the hydrodynamic field of
f_{n}
is defined to be

[\rho_{n},u_{n},T_{n}]
,
which represents the density, velocity, and temperature fluctuations physically.

1.3.
Solutions of fluid equations

Formally, by the first two equations of (
1.8
), we see that the leading order
\mathbf{M}
of the Hilbert expansion of the Landau equation (
1.1
) gives the compressible Euler equations:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+\nabla_{x}\cdot(\rho u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}[\rho u]+\nabla_{x}\cdot[\rho u\otimes u]+\nabla_{x}p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(1.26)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left[\rho\left(\mathbf{E}+\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}\right)\right]+\nabla_{x}\cdot\left[\rho u\left(\mathbf{E}+\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}\right)\right]+\nabla_{x}(pu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

with the equation of state

p=\rho RT=\frac{2}{3}\rho\mathbf{E},

where
\mathbf{E}
is the internal energy. Recalling that we take
R=\frac{2}{3}
in this paper, we can thus conclude that
\mathbf{E}=T
.

Similarly, by setting
T=\mathbf{E}=\rho^{2/3}
, the leading order of the Hilbert expansion (
1.19
) for the VML system (
1.2
) yields the following compressible Euler-Maxwell system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+\nabla_{x}\cdot(\rho u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}[\rho u]+\nabla_{x}\cdot\left(\rho u\otimes u\right)+\frac{2}{3}\nabla_{x}\rho^{\frac{5}{3}}+\rho\big(E+u\times B\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E-\nabla_{x}\times B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\pi\rho u,

(1.27)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}B+\nabla_{x}\times E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\pi\big(1-\rho\big),

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

Now we state some well-established well-posedness results on the Cauchy problems of the Euler equations (
1.3
) and the Euer-Maxwell system (
1.3
).

In fact, for the local smooth solvability of the Euler equations (
1.3
) with prescribed initial datum

\displaystyle[\rho,u,T](0,x)=[\rho_{0},u_{0},T_{0}](x),

(1.28)

we have the following result, cf.
[
48
, Theorem 2.1, pp.30]
.

Proposition 1.1

(Local solvability of the Euler system)
.

Assume
[\rho_{0}-1,u_{0},T_{0}-1]\in H^{s}
with
s>\frac{5}{2}
and

\inf\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho_{0}(x)>0,\ \ \inf\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}T_{0}(x)>0.

Moreover, let

\epsilon_{0}=\|[\rho_{0}-1,u_{0},T_{0}-1]\|_{H^{s}}.

Then there exists a time interval
[0,t_{e}]
with
t_{e}>0
such that the Cauchy problem (
1.3
) and (
1.28
) admits a unique smooth solution
[\rho,u,\theta](t,x)\in C^{1}([0,t_{e}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
with

\inf\limits_{(t,x)\in[0,t_{e}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho(t,x)>0,\ \ \inf\limits_{(t,x)\in[0,t_{e}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}T(t,x)>0

and
t_{e}
depending on
\|[\rho_{0}-1,u_{0},T_{0}-1]\|_{H^{s}}
,
\inf\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho_{0}(x)
and
\inf\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}T_{0}(x)
. Furthermore, there exists a constant
C(t_{e})>0
independent of
\epsilon_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,t_{e}]}\|[\rho(t,x)-1,u(t,x),T(t,x)-1]\|_{H^{s}}\leq C(t_{e})\epsilon_{0}.

(1.29)

For the Cauchy problem of the Euler-Maxwell system (
1.3
) with initial datum

\displaystyle[\rho,u,T,E,B](0,x)=[\rho_{0},u_{0},T_{0},E_{0},B_{0}](x),\ T_{0}=\rho_{0}^{2/3},

(1.30)

it is shown in
[
35
, Theorem 1.3, pp. 2359]
that such a Cauchy problem does exists a global smooth solution
[\rho(t,x),u(t,x),E(t,x),B(t,x)]
.

Proposition 1.2

(Global solvability of the Euler-Maxwell system)
.

Let
N_{0}>10^{4}
be a sufficiently large integer and denote

\left\|\left[\rho_{0}-1,u_{0},T_{0}-1,E_{0},B_{0}\right]\right\|_{H^{N_{0}+1}}+\left\|(\mathbf{I}-\Delta)^{1/2}u_{0}\right\|_{Z}+\left\|(\mathbf{I}-\Delta)^{1/2}E_{0}\right\|_{Z}=\epsilon_{1},

where the norm
\|\cdot\|_{Z}
is defined by
[
35
, (2.18), pp. 2371]
.

Assume that

\rho_{0}=-\nabla_{x}\cdot E_{0},\ B_{0}=\nabla_{x}\times u_{0},

and
\epsilon_{1}>0
is chosen suitably small, there exists a unique global smooth solution
[\rho(t,x),u(t,x),

T(t,x),E(t,x),B(t,x)]
with
T(t,x)=\rho(t,x)^{2/3}
to the Cauchy problem (
1.3
) and (
1.30
) which satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\infty]}\left\|\left[\rho(t,x)-1,T(t,x)-1,u(t,x),E(t,x),B(t,x)\right]\right\|_{H^{N_{0}+1}}

(1.31)

\displaystyle+\sup_{t\in[0,\infty]}\left[(1+t)^{p_{0}}\sup_{i\leq N_{1}}\left(\left\|\nabla_{x}^{i}[\rho(t,x)-1]\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|\nabla_{x}^{i}[T(t,x)-1]\right\|_{\infty}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\sup_{t\in[0,\infty]}\Big[(1+t)^{p_{0}}\sup_{i\leq N_{1}+1}\left\|\left[\nabla_{x}^{i}u(t,x),\nabla_{x}^{i}E(t,x),\nabla_{x}^{i}B(t,x)\right]\right\|_{\infty}\Big]\leq C\epsilon_{1},

where
3\leq N_{1}\ll N_{0}
,
p_{0}>1
,
C
is some positive constant independent of
\epsilon_{1}
.

From the estimates (
1.29
) and (
1.31
), it is seen that for any given generic positive constant
C_{0}\geq 1
, if both
\epsilon_{0}
and
\epsilon_{1}
are chosen suitably small, we can deduce that the solutions obtained in Propositions
1.1
and
1.2
satisfy the following property

Corollary 1.1
.

For any given generic positive constant
C_{0}\geq 1
, if we assume further that
\epsilon_{0}
and
\epsilon_{1}
are sufficiently small, then one can always find a positive constant
T_{c}
such that either

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{C_{0}}\leq T(t,x)-T_{c}\leq C_{0}\epsilon_{0}

(1.32)

holds for all
(t,x)\in[0,t_{e}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}

or

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon_{1}}{C_{0}}\leq T(t,x)-T_{c}\leq C_{0}\epsilon_{1}

(1.33)

holds for all
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

1.4.
Main results

In this subsection, we shall present our main results. Our first main result is concerned with the rigorously justification of the local validity of the Hilbert expansion of the Landau equation (
1.1
). Before stating our result, we first define the following energy functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{2}\varepsilon^{i}\left(\left\|\nabla_{x}^{i}f^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|\nabla_{x}^{i}h^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}\right)

(1.34)

and the following energy dissipation rate functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(t)\backsimeq\sum_{i=0}^{2}\varepsilon^{i-1}\left(\left\|\nabla_{x}^{i}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})[f^{\varepsilon}](t)\right\|^{2}_{D}+\varepsilon\left\|\nabla_{x}^{i}({\bf I}-{\bf P})[h^{\varepsilon}](t)\right\|^{2}_{D}\right),

(1.35)

then our first result can be stated as follows.

Theorem 1.1

(Local Hilbert expansion of the Landau equation)
.

Assume that

•

The initial daum
F^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x,v)
satisfies

F^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x,v)=\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}F_{n,0}(x,v)+\varepsilon^{k}F^{\varepsilon}_{R,0}(x,v)\geq 0

and
[\rho(t,x),u(t,x),T(t,x)]
is a smooth solution of the Euler equation (
1.3
) constructed in Proposition
1.1
;

•

T_{c}>0
is suitably chosen such that
(
1.32
)
holds for all
(t,x)\in[0,t_{e}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
;

•

There exists constant
C>0
such that for
N\geq 2

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\alpha_{0}+|\alpha|\leq N+4k-2n+2}

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}^{\alpha_{0}}\partial^{\alpha}\left[\rho_{n,0},u_{n,0},T_{n,0}\right](x)\right\|\leq C,\ \ 1\leq n\leq 2k-1;

(1.36)

•

k\geq 3
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(0)\lesssim 1,

then there exists a constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, the Cauchy problem of the Landau equation (
1.1
) and (
1.4
) admits a unique smooth solution
F^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)
which satisfies

F^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)=\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}F_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}F^{\varepsilon}_{R}\geq 0

in the time interval
[0,t_{e}]
. Moreover, for any
t\in[0,t_{e}]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{e}}\left\|{\mathbf{M}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(t)\left(F^{\varepsilon}(t)-\mathbf{M}(t)\right)\right\|_{H^{2}}=0,

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(s)\,ds\lesssim\mathcal{E}(0)+\varepsilon^{2k+3}.

(1.37)

Our second result in the article is concerned with the Hilbert expansion of the VML system (
1.2
).
As (
1.34
) and (
1.35
), we first define
the energy functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{2}\varepsilon^{i}\left[\left(\left\|\sqrt{4\pi RT}\nabla_{x}^{i}f^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}+\left\|\nabla_{x}^{i}E_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}+\left\|\nabla_{x}^{i}B_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}\right)+\varepsilon^{\frac{4}{3}}\left\|w_{i}\nabla_{x}^{i}h^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}\right],

(1.38)

and the dissipation rate functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(t)\backsimeq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{2}\varepsilon^{i}\left[\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\|\nabla_{x}^{i}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})\left[f^{\varepsilon}\right](t)\right\|^{2}_{D}+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{3}}\left\|w_{i}\nabla_{x}^{i}h^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}_{D}\right.

(1.39)

\displaystyle\left.+\varepsilon^{\frac{4}{3}}Y(t)\left\|(1+|v|)w_{i}\nabla_{x}^{i}h^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}\right],

then our main result can be stated as follows

Theorem 1.2

(Global Hilbert expansion of the VML system)
.

Assume that

•

The initial daum
[F^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x,v),E^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x),B^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)]
satisfies

\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}F_{n,0}+\varepsilon^{k}F^{\varepsilon}_{R,0}\geq 0,

and

\displaystyle E^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}E_{n,0}+\varepsilon^{k}E^{\varepsilon}_{R,0},\ B^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}B_{n,0}+\varepsilon^{k}B^{\varepsilon}_{R,0},

and
[\rho(t,x),u(t,x)
,
T(t,x),E(t,x),B(t,x)]

is a smooth solution constructed in Proposition
1.2
for the Euler-Maxwell system (
1.3
);

•

T_{c}
is suitably chosen such that there exists a positive constant
C_{0}>1

(
1.33
)
is valid
for all
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
;

•

There exists constant
C>0
such that for
N\geq 2

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\alpha_{0}+|\alpha|\leq N+4k-2n+2}

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}^{\alpha_{0}}\partial^{\alpha}[\rho_{n,0},u_{n,0},T_{n,0},E_{n,0},B_{n,0}](x)\right\|\leq C,\ \ 1\leq n\leq 2k-1;

(1.40)

•

k\geq 3
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(0)\lesssim 1,

then there exists a constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such
that for
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, the Cauchy problem of the VML system (
1.2
) and (
1.5
) admits a unique smooth solution

[F^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v),E^{\varepsilon}(t,x),B^{\varepsilon}(t,x)]
satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &F^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}F_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}F^{\varepsilon}_{R}\geq 0,\quad E^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}E_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}E^{\varepsilon}_{R},\quad B^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}B_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}B^{\varepsilon}_{R}.\end{aligned}

Furthermore, it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\sup_{0\leq t\leq\varepsilon^{-1/3}}\left\|\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(t)\left(F^{\varepsilon}(t)-\mathbf{M}(t)\right)\right\|_{H^{2}}=0

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(s)\,ds\lesssim\mathcal{E}(0)+1,\ 0\leq t\leq\varepsilon^{-1/3}.

(1.41)

The following remark is concerned with the initial condition of the coefficients
F_{n}
(1\leq n\leq 2k-1)
in the Hilbert expansion.

Remark 1.2
.

The

t-
derivatives in (
1.36
) and (
1.40
) are understood through the moment equations of evolution equations (
1.8
) and (
1.20
), for instance,
\partial_{t}[\rho_{n,0},u_{n,0},T_{n,0},E_{n,0},B_{n,0}](x)
is given by (
4.1
). It should also be pointed out that only the initial data of the macroscopic part of the coefficients are imposed because the microscopic component is determined by iteration relation in (
1.36
) and (
1.40
).

1.5.
Literatures

There have been many results on the link between the kinetic equations and the systems of fluid dynamics (i.e., Euler or Navier-Stokes equations). It turns out that the relations between the fluid-dynamic picture and the kinetic description can be explored from several mathematical points of view. One of those is to study the equivalence of kinetic equations and fluid systems in the time-asymptotic sense, see
[
40
]
, particularly we refer to
[
7
,
10
,
34
,
44
,
47
]
for the study of this equivalence from the perspective of dissipative waves.

Another available strategy is to study the small Knudsen number limits of the kinetic equations. Generally speaking, there are also some different kinds of program to investigate such kinds of approximation. One is based on the Chapman-Enskog procedure
[
5
]
, the first order approximation of which gives the compressible Euler equations and the second order approximation is the compressible Navier-Stokes system. Along this direction, Nishida
[
50
]
, Ukai-Asano
[
57
]
proved this compressible Euler approximation by semigroup method in the framework of analytical function space. Recently, such a validity has been established near a shock wave
[
60
]
, rarefaction wave
[
58
]
, contact wave
[
31
]
and the composition of the basic wave patterns
[
32
,
33
]
. We also mention that the validity of the Chapman-Enskog expansion of the Bolzmann equation to the solution of compressible Navier-Stokes system was studied in
[
43
]
and was recently justified by Liu-Yang-Zhao
[
46
]
for the Cauchy problem and by Duan-Liu
[
8
]
for initial-boundary value problem with physical boundary conditions. The second program mainly developed by Bardos-Golse-Levermore is concerned with the incompressible fluid limits via the renormalized solutions, entropy and weak convergence method. We refer to Bardos-Golse-Levermore
[
2
,
3
]
, Golse-Saint-Raymond
[
18
,
19
]
, Jiang-Masmoudi
[
39
]
, Lions-Masmoudi
[
45
]
, Masmoudi-Saint-Raymond
[
49
]
, Saint-Raymond
[
54
]
. Moreover, Guo
[
22
]
discussed the incompressible Navier-Stokes-Fourier limits of both the Boltzmann equation and Landau equation via diffusive expansion in the framework of classical solutions.

To justify the small Knudsen number limits of the kinetic equations rigorously, the third approach is based on the Hilbert expansion. For results in this direction, the validity of the Hilbert expansion of the Bolzmann equation in one dimension to the classical solutions of the compressible Euler equations in a finite time, was justified by Caflisch
[
4
]
via a robust decomposition of the remainder equation. Later on, Lachowicz studied the multidimensional case including the initial layer in Sobolev space
[
41
]
and also in
C^{0}
space
[
42
]
. Around 2010, the compressible Euler limits and acoustic limits as well as the nonnegativity of solution of the Boltzmann equation were justified by Guo-Jang-Jiang
[
27
,
28
]
with the aid of a new
\varepsilon
-dependent energy functional and an
L^{2}-L^{\infty}
approach developed in
[
23
]
. When the physical boundary is also taken into consideration, Esposito-Lebowitz-Marra
[
16
,
17
]
investigated the Hilbert expansion for the stationary Boltzmann equation in a slab with diffusive reflection boundary condition. Very recently, Guo-Huang-Wang
[
25
]
, Jang-kim
[
36
]
and Jiang-Luo-Tang
[
37
,
38
]
studied the half space problem with physical boundary conditions. It should be pointed out that the Hilbert expansion requires the existence of smooth solution of the compressible Euler type equations, thus the solution of the Boltzmann equation is obtained before the shock formations in the compressible Euler flow. For the more complicate model Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann (VPB for short in the sequel) system, based on
[
20
]
, Guo-Jang
[
26
]
proved that the Hilbert expansion of VPB is valid for all time in the
L^{2}-L^{\infty}
setting, which implies the global in time convergence from the VPB system to the Euler-Poisson system. Recently, Arsénio-Saint-Raymond
[
1
]
discussed the magnetohydrodynamic limits of the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system by mainly using some generalized relative entropy method.

Although the well-posedness of the Landau equation has already been established cf.
[
21
,
9
]
, much less is known about the validity of the Hilbert expansion of the Landau equation, let alone the VML system, which is due to the severe singularity of the large velocities and differential dissipation structure of the Landau collision operator. We further mention that the stability of contact wave of Landau equation was proved by Duan-Yang-Yu
[
11
]
and the global classical solution of the VML system was constructed in
[
6
,
59
]
. Recently, Rachid
[
53
]
investigated the incompressible fluid limits of the Landau equation in a weak convergence regime, Duan-Yang-Yu validate the Chapman-Enskog expansion of the Landau equation
[
12
]
and the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system
[
13
]
in a perturbative framework via an energy method and a careful analysis of the Burnett function. In the case of relativistic models, Speck-Strain
[
56
]
studied the local-in-time hydrodynamic limit of the relativistic Boltzmann equation using a Hilbert expansion, and in a series of papers, Guo-Xiao showed the validity of the Hilbert expansion for the relativistic Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system
[
29
]
by the generalized
L^{2}-L^{\infty}
approach and Ouyang-Wu-Xiao discussed the the Hilbert expansion for both the relativistic Landau equation
[
51
]
and the relativistic VML system
[
52
]
by an energy method with exponential momentum weights. In this paper, we aim to justify the compressible fluid approximation of both the Landau equation and the VML system in the setting of Hilbert expansion. To the best of our knowledge, our paper appears to be the first result to justify the validity of the Hilbert expansion for such Landau type equations.

1.6.
Strategies and ideas

One typical difficulty in the rigorous justification of the Hilbert expansion for non-relativistic kinetic equations in perturbative regime is the large velocity growth caused by the leading order
\mathbf{M}_{[\rho,u,T]}
, which leads to
|v|^{3}
growth after acted by transport operator, namely

\displaystyle\partial^{\alpha}f^{\varepsilon}\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\big)\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\left\|\partial_{t,x}[\rho,u,T]\right\|_{L^{\infty}}(1+|v|)^{3}\left|\partial^{\alpha}f^{\varepsilon}\right|.

(1.42)

For the Boltzmann type equations with cut-off potentials, to overcome such a difficulty, Caflisch
[
4
]
and Guo-Jang-Jiang
[
27
,
26
]
introduced the global Maxwellian
\mu
defined by (
1.14
) with
T_{c}<T(t,x)
, and by making full use of the nice structures and properties of the Boltzmann collision operator with cut-off potentials, they succeeded in justifying rigorously the validity of the Hilbert expansion of these equations. As far as we know, it is still an interesting and challenging problem to see whether the approaches developed in
[
4
,
27
,
26
]
can be adapted to deal with the same problem for the Landau type equations and the Boltzmann type equations for long-range interactions or not.

While for the relativistic Landau type equations, the resulting difficult term corresponding to (
1.42
) leads to linear growth in momentum only. Based on such an observation, Ouyang-Wu-Xiao
[
51
,
52
]
proved the validity of the Hilbert expansion for the relativistic Landau equation and the relativistic VML system by introducing an exponentially weighted energy method around the local Maxwellians

\bf M
.

Motivated by these works, the main idea in this paper is to introduce an energy method around both the local Maxwellian
\mathbf{M}
and global Maxwellian
\mu
by setting

\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon}_{R}=\sqrt{\mathbf{M}}f^{\varepsilon}=\sqrt{\mu}h^{\varepsilon}\ \textrm{with}\ T_{c}<T,

(1.43)

and the key point in our analysis is to make use of an interplay of the energy estimates for
f^{\varepsilon}
and
h^{\varepsilon}
such that we can close the energy estimates, from which the wellposedness of the remainder
F_{R}^{\varepsilon}
can be established in Sobolev space.

Now we first outline the whole picture of strategies employed in our proof for the local validity of the Hilbert expansion of the Landau equation (
1.1
).

For the energy estimates around the local Maxwellian
\mathbf{M}
, that is the energy type estimates of
f^{\varepsilon}
, to deal with the problematic term corresponding to (
1.42
), we divide
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}
into the low velocity part and the high velocity part. For the low velocity part, we can bound its microscopic part and macroscopic part by
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\|\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})[f^{\varepsilon}]\|_{D}^{2}
and
\|f^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}}
, respectively; while for the high velocity part, noticing that

\displaystyle\left|\partial^{\alpha}f^{\varepsilon}\right|\lesssim\sum_{j=0}^{i}{\langle v\rangle}^{2(|\alpha|-j)}\exp\left(-\frac{(T-T_{c})|v|^{2}}{8RTT_{c}}\right)\left|\partial_{x}^{j}h^{\varepsilon}\right|,\quad 0\leq|\alpha|\leq 2,

(1.44)

which is a direct consequence of the assumption that
\mathbf{M}
and
\mu
are sufficiently close, we can control it via
\|h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}}
.

To derive the desired estimates of
h^{\varepsilon}
, we need to estimate the term
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big|\big\langle\partial^{\alpha}\mathcal{L}_{d}[h^{\varepsilon}],\partial^{\alpha}h^{\varepsilon}\big\rangle\big|
, which can be estimated as follows

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big|\big\langle\partial^{\alpha}\mathcal{L}_{d}[h^{\varepsilon}],\partial^{\alpha}h^{\varepsilon}\big\rangle\big|\lesssim\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{\varepsilon}\big\|h^{\varepsilon}\big\|_{H^{|\alpha|}_{D}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{\varepsilon}\Big(\big\|({\bf I}-{\bf P})[h^{\varepsilon}]\|_{H^{|\alpha|}_{D}}^{2}+\big\|{\bf P}[h^{\varepsilon}]\|_{H^{|\alpha|}_{D}}^{2}\Big),

(1.45)

but the macroscopic component
\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{\varepsilon}\big\|{\bf P}[h^{\varepsilon}]\|_{H^{|\alpha|}_{D}}^{2}
is out of control due to the
\varepsilon
-singularity. To overcome such a difficulty, our another contribution of this paper is to use
f^{\varepsilon}
to dominate this trouble term because one can check that

\displaystyle\left\|{\bf P}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\right]\right\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}_{D}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\|{\bf P}\left[f^{\varepsilon}\right]\right\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}_{D}}\lesssim\left\|f^{\varepsilon}\right\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}}

(1.46)

by using (
1.43
) again.

Now we explain our strategy to make use of the interplay of the estimates
\left\|f^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|_{H^{2}}
and
\left\|h^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|_{H^{2}}
to yield the desired estimates. In fact, when performing the energy estimates on
f^{\varepsilon}
, as observed in
[
51
,
52
]
, since the linear collision operator term
\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}[f^{\varepsilon}]}{\varepsilon}
and
{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}[f^{\varepsilon}]
do not commute with the spatial derivative operator
\partial^{\alpha}
, singularities
\varepsilon^{-|\alpha|}
appear in the estimate of
\|\partial^{\alpha}f^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}
. Similarly, as a consequence of (
1.45
) and (
1.46
), when we deduce an estimate on
\|h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}}
, the term
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\|f^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}}
with
the singular factor
\varepsilon^{-1}
must appear in its upper bound estimates. To remove these singularities, we equip norms
\|\partial^{\alpha}f^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}
with an coefficient
\varepsilon^{|\alpha|}
for
0\leq|\alpha|\leq 2
and multiply the norms
\|\partial^{{\alpha}}h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}
by a weight
\varepsilon^{|\alpha|+1}
to yield the desired energy type estimates. This is the very reason for our introduction of the energy functional
\mathcal{E}(t)
defined in (
1.34
). By such an interplay of the energy estimates
\|f^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{2}}
and
\|h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{2}}
around both the local Maxwellian
\mathbf{M}
and the global Maxwellian
\mu
, we finally arrive at a closed
a priori
estimates.

Compared with the local validity of the Hilbert expansion for the Landau equation (
1.1
), the problem on the global Hilbert expansion of the VML system (
1.2
) is more complicated and the main difficulties are two-folds: the first one is due to the presence of the Lorentz force terms, while the another is that we need to show that the Hilbert expansion is valid globally. Our main ideas to solve the first problem can be summarized as in the following:

•

For the energy estimates of
f^{\varepsilon}
, the biggest difficulty comes from terms like
-(E+v\times B)\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{\varepsilon}
which include velocity growth and velocity derivatives. These terms can be estimated in an analogous way as the problematic term (
1.42
). In fact, we can also obtain upper bound estimates for
\partial^{\alpha}\nabla_{v}f^{\varepsilon}
similar to (
1.44
). And for the high velocity part of the corresponding velocity weighted norm estimates of
\partial^{\alpha}\nabla_{v}f^{\varepsilon}
, it can be controlled via
\|h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}}
and
\|h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}_{D}}
for
0\leq|\alpha|\leq 2
.

•

For estimates of the remainder
h^{\varepsilon}
, except for the problems mentioned in the case of the Landau equation (
1.1
), there are some additional difficulties. The first one stems from the interaction between particles and electromagnetic field, such as
(E+v\times B)\cdot\nabla_{v}h^{\varepsilon}
,
\frac{E\cdot v}{2RT_{c}}h^{\varepsilon}
, etc., and these terms include velocity growth and derivatives and thus cannot be controlled directly in the energy estimates.
To control the term
(E+v\times B)\cdot\nabla_{v}h^{\varepsilon}
, as in
[
24
]
, a polynomial weight depending on the order of the
x-
derivatives is designed to alleviate the velocity growth, while for the more delicate term
\frac{E\cdot v}{2RT_{c}}h^{\varepsilon}
, it leads to temporal decay but velocity growth in the form of

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}(1+t)^{-p_{0}}\|\sqrt{{\langle v\rangle}}w_{0}h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}

(1.47)

for some
p_{0}>1
, which is also beyond control. To overcome such a difficulty, following the technique developed in
[
14
,
15
]
, we introduce the time-dependent exponential weight function
\exp\left(\frac{(1+|v|^{2})}{8RT_{c}\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)}\right)
while performing the energy estimates, because this weight function gives the time degenerate damping

\frac{1}{8RT_{c}(\mathrm{e}+t)\big(\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)\big)^{2}}\|{\langle v\rangle}w_{0}h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}.

From which and thanks to the good temporal-decay of solutions to the Euler-Maxwell system (
1.3
), we can then control (
1.47
) suitably.

As to the second problem, although generally speaking, we can only deduce that the coefficients
[F_{n},E_{n},B_{n}]
enjoy the polynomial temporal growth for
1\leq n\leq 2k-1
, by employing the nice temporal-decay properties of solutions to the Euler-Maxwell system (
1.3
), we can kill the above mentioned time growth via corresponding power of
\varepsilon
and prove that the Hilbert expansion of the VML system (
1.2
) is valid for
t\leq\varepsilon^{-1/3}
. It is worth to pointing out that, to guarantee that
\|f^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}}
is integrable over
t\leq\varepsilon^{-1/3}
, compared with the design of the energy functional
\mathcal{E}(t)
to the Landau equation (
1.1
) defined by (
1.34
), an additional weight
\varepsilon^{1/3}
should be added to the norms
\|h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}}
to kill the time growth. With the well-designed energy functional
\mathcal{E}(t)
given by (
1.38
), we arrive at a closed
a priori
estimates by an interplay of the energy estimates
\|f^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{2}}
and
\|wh^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{2}}
around both the local Maxwellian
\mathbf{M}
and the global Maxwellian
\mu
, respectively.

Our paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we collect some estimates concerning the collision operators
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}
,
\mathcal{L}_{d}
and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}
defined in (
1.12
), (
1.18
) and (
1.13
), respectively. Section
3
is devoted to justifying the local validity of the Hilbert expansion (
1.6
) for the Cauchy problem (
1.1
), (
1.4
) of the Landau equation (
1.1
), while the rigorous justification of the global Hilbert expansion (
1.19
) of the Cauchy problem (
1.2
), (
1.5
) of the VML system (
1.2
) will be given in Section
4
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we collect some estimates concerning the collision operators
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}
,
\mathcal{L}_{d}
and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}
defined in (
1.12
), (
1.18
) and (
1.13
), respectively.
To do this, we first give the following explicit expressions of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}
,
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{d}
,
\mathcal{K}_{d}
and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

It holds that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rll}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}[f]=&\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\partial_{j}f\Big]-\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\frac{(v_{i}-u_{i})(v_{j}-u_{j})}{4R^{2}T^{2}}f+\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}\Big]f,\\[5.69054pt]
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}[f]=&-\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\mathbf{M}\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}f+\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}\Big)\Big)\Big],\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rll}\mathcal{A}_{d}[h]=&\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\Big(\frac{-u_{j}}{RT}+\frac{T_{c}-T}{RT_{c}T}v_{j}\Big)h\Big]-\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\frac{v_{i}}{2RT_{c}}\Big(\frac{-u_{j}}{RT}+\frac{T_{c}-T}{RT_{c}T}v_{j}\Big)h\\[5.69054pt]
&+\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\partial_{j}h\Big]-\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\frac{v_{i}v_{j}}{4R^{2}T_{c}T}f+\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\frac{v_{j}}{2RT_{c}}\Big]h,\\[5.69054pt]
\mathcal{K}_{d}[h]=&\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\Big(\frac{u_{j}}{RT}\mathbf{M}+\frac{(T-T_{c})\mu-T_{c}(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}{RT_{c}T}v_{j}\Big)\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h\Big)\Big]\\[5.69054pt]
&-\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\big(\mathbf{M}-\mu\big)\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}h-\frac{v_{j}}{2RT_{c}}h\Big)\Big)\Big],\end{array}\right.

and

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f_{1},f_{2})=

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\Big[\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{1}\Big)\partial_{j}f_{2}-\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{j}f_{1}\Big)f_{2}\Big]

\displaystyle-\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{1}\Big)\partial_{j}f_{2}+\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{j}f_{1}\Big)f_{2},

where
\partial_{i}=\partial_{v_{i}}

(i=1,2,3)
and

\sigma^{ij}_{g}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\phi^{ij}(v-v^{\prime})g(v^{\prime})dv^{\prime}.

Proof.

The proof will be done in a similar way as in
[
21
, Lemma 1, pp. 395]
. For brevity, we only show (
2.1
).
For this purpose, direct calculation gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{d}[h]=

\displaystyle\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{M}-\mu,\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}h-\frac{v_{j}}{2RT_{c}}h\Big)-\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\Big(\frac{-v_{j}+u_{j}}{RT}\mathbf{M}+\frac{v_{j}}{RT_{c}}\mu\Big)\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\Big(\frac{-u_{j}}{RT}+\frac{T_{c}-T}{RT_{c}T}v_{j}\Big)\mathbf{M}\Big)\Big]+\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\Big(\partial_{j}h+\frac{v_{j}}{2RT_{c}}h\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\Big(\frac{-u_{j}}{RT}+\frac{T_{c}-T}{RT_{c}T}v_{j}\Big)h\Big]-\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\frac{v_{i}}{2RT_{c}}\Big(\frac{-u_{j}}{RT}+\frac{T_{c}-T}{RT_{c}T}v_{j}\Big)h

\displaystyle+\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\partial_{j}h\Big]-\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\frac{v_{i}v_{j}}{4R^{2}T_{c}T}h+\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\frac{v_{j}}{2RT_{c}}\Big]h

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{d}[h]=

\displaystyle\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{C}(\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h,\mathbf{M}-\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h\Big)\Big(\frac{-v_{j}+u_{j}}{RT}\mathbf{M}+\frac{v_{j}}{RT_{c}}\mu\Big)

\displaystyle-\big(\mathbf{M}-\mu\big)\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}h-\frac{v_{j}}{2RT_{c}}h\Big)\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\Big(\frac{u_{j}}{RT}\mathbf{M}+\frac{(T-T_{c})\mu-T_{c}(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}{RT_{c}T}v_{j}\Big)\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle-\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\big(\mathbf{M}-\mu\big)\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}h-\frac{v_{j}}{2RT_{c}}h\Big)\Big)\Big].

This is exactly (
2.1
) and the proof of Lemma
2.1
is complete.
∎

The following lemma is quoted from
[
21
, Corollary 1, pp. 399]
, which states the lower bound of
D-
norm defined by (
1.23
).

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
D-
norm be defined as (
1.23
), then
there exists
C>0
such that

|g|_{D}^{2}\geq C\left\{\left|(1+|v|)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\{{\bf P}_{v}\partial_{j}g\}\right|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left|(1+|v|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\{({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{v})\partial_{j}g\}\right|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left|(1+|v|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}g\right|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right\},

where
{\bf P}_{v}
is the projection defined by

{\bf P}_{v}h_{j}=\sum{h_{k}v_{k}}\frac{v_{j}}{|v|^{2}},\ \ 1\leq j\leq 3,

for any vector-valued function
h(v)=[h_{1}(v),h_{2}(v),h_{3}(v)]
.

We now give the coercivity estimates of the linearized operator
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}
and the upper bound of the difference operator
\mathcal{L}_{d}.

Lemma 2.3
.

The linearized operator
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}
satisfy the following coercivity estimates:

(i)

Let
\mathcal{L}
be given by (
1.17
), it holds that

\big\langle\mathcal{L}h,h\big\rangle\gtrsim|({\bf I}-{\bf P})h|_{D}^{2}.

(2.7)

(ii)

Let
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}_{[\rho,u,T]},
where
[\rho,u,T]
is determined by either Proposition
1.1
or Proposition
1.2
, then there exists a
\delta>0
such that

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}f,f\big)\geq\delta\big|({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f\big|_{D}^{2}.

(2.8)

Moreover, for
|\beta|>0
,
\ell\geq 0
and
q\in[0,1)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\big(\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}f,\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial_{\beta}^{\alpha}f\big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\delta\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{D}^{2}-\big(\eta+C\bar{\epsilon}\big)\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha}\sum_{|\beta^{\prime}|=|\beta|}\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta^{\prime}|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}f\big|_{D}^{2}

(2.9)

\displaystyle-C(\eta)\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha}\sum_{|\beta^{\prime}|<|\beta|}\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta^{\prime}|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}f\big|^{2}_{D},

where
\bar{\epsilon}=\max\{\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1}\}
,
and for
|\beta|=0
,
\ell\geq 0
and
q\in[0,1)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\big(\partial^{\alpha}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}f,\langle v\rangle^{2\ell}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}f\big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\delta\big|\langle v\rangle^{\ell}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}f\big|_{D}^{2}-C\bar{\epsilon}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha}{\bf 1}_{|\alpha|\geq 1}\big|\langle v\rangle^{\ell}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}h\big|_{D}^{2}

(2.10)

\displaystyle-C(\eta)\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha}\big|\langle v\rangle^{\ell}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}f\big|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{C}(\eta))}.

Here
\eta>0
is some small constant,
B_{C}(\eta)
is the ball in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with radius
C(\eta)
.

Furthermore, for
|\alpha|>0
, there exists a polynomial
\mathcal{U}(\cdot)
with
\mathcal{U}(0)=0
such that

\displaystyle\big\langle\partial^{\alpha}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}f,\partial^{\alpha}f\big\rangle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{3\delta}{4}\|\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f\|_{D}^{2}-C\mathcal{U}(\|\nabla_{x}[\rho,u,T]\|_{W^{|\alpha|-1,\infty}})

(2.11)

\displaystyle\times\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\|({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f\|_{H^{|\alpha|-1}_{D}}^{2}+\varepsilon\|f\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}}\Big).

Proof.

(
2.7
) is directly from
[
21
, Lemma 5, pp. 400]
. Since
[\rho,u,T]
satisfies either (
1.29
) or (
1.31
), (
2.8
) follows again from a similar argument as in the proof of
[
21
, Lemma 5, pp. 400]
.

We now turn to prove (
(ii)
). Recalling
-\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}+\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}
with
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}
being given by (
2.1
), direct calculation gives

\displaystyle\big(\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}f,\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{\tilde{D}}^{2}

\displaystyle-{\bf 1}_{\alpha+\beta>0}\sum\limits_{0<\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}\leq\alpha+\beta}C_{\alpha}^{\alpha^{\prime}}C_{\beta}^{\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\partial^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{j}f,\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\partial_{i}f\big)

\displaystyle-\sum\limits_{\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha,\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta}C_{\alpha}^{\alpha^{\prime}}C_{\beta}^{\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{i}\left(\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\right)\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\partial^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{j}f,\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\partial_{i}f\big)

\displaystyle-{\bf 1}_{\alpha+\beta>0}\sum\limits_{0<\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}\leq\alpha+\beta}C_{\alpha}^{\alpha^{\prime}}C_{\beta}^{\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}\left(\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\frac{(v_{i}-u_{i})(v_{j}-u_{j})}{4R^{2}T^{2}}\right)\partial^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{j}f,\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\partial_{i}f\big)

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}\leq\alpha+\beta}C_{\alpha}^{\alpha^{\prime}}C_{\beta}^{\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}\left(\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}\right)\partial^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{j}f,\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\partial_{i}f\big),

(2.12)

where

\displaystyle\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{\tilde{D}}^{2}=

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\sigma_{\mathbf{M}}^{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}f\partial_{v_{j}}f\,dv

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4R^{2}T^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\sigma_{\mathbf{M}}^{ij}(v_{i}-u_{i})(v_{j}-u_{j})|f|^{2}\,dv.

Noting that
[\rho,u,T]
satisfies either (
1.32
) or (
1.33
), one gets by denoting
\bar{\epsilon}=\max\{\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1}\}
that

\displaystyle\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{\tilde{D}}^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{D}^{2}-C\bar{\epsilon}\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{D}^{2}.

Next, by using (
1.32
) and (
1.33
) and by performing the similar calculation as in the proof of
[
55
, Lemma 8, pp. 319]
, we see that the rest terms in the right hand side of (
2.12
)
can be dominated by

\displaystyle\big(\eta+C\bar{\epsilon}\big)\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha}\sum_{|\beta^{\prime}|=|\beta|}\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta^{\prime}|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}f\big|_{D}^{2}+C(\eta)\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha}\sum_{|\beta^{\prime}|<|\beta|}\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta^{\prime}|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}f\big|^{2}_{D},

where
0<\eta\ll 1.

Now for the estimates involving
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}
, we first have from (
2.1
) that

\displaystyle\big(\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}f,\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(\partial_{i}\left[\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\left\{\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}f+\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}f\Big)\Big)\right\}\right],\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\partial_{i}\left[\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\right]\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\left\{\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}f+\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}f\Big)\Big)\right\},\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\left\{\frac{v_{i}-u_{i}}{2RT}\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}f+\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}f\Big)\Big)\right\},\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\right).

Since for any finite
k\in\mathbb{N}
and any multi-indices
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},
it follows from (
1.29
) and (
1.31
) that

\nabla_{v}^{k}\left\{\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\right\}\mathbf{M}^{1/2}\leq C\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1-q}{4}},\ \partial_{\beta^{\prime}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\left\{\frac{v_{i}-u_{i}}{2RT}\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}\leq C\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{4}},

one has by using the same argument as for obtaining the estimates on
K
in
[
55
, Lemma 8, pp. 319]
that

\displaystyle\big(\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}f,\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\{\eta\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{D}+\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}f\big|_{L^{2}(B_{C}(\eta))}\right\}\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{D}.

Putting the above estimates for
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}
together, we see that (
(ii)
) is true.
(
(ii)
) can be proved similarly as above.

As for (
2.11
), applying (
2.8
), we get

\displaystyle\big\langle\partial^{\alpha}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}f,\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f,({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})\partial^{\alpha}f\big\rangle+\big\langle\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\Rbrack({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f,\partial^{\alpha}f\big\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f,\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f+\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},{{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}}\Rbrack f\big\rangle+\big\langle\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\Rbrack({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f,\partial^{\alpha}f\big\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\delta\|\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f\|_{D}^{2}+\big\langle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f,\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},{{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}}\Rbrack f\big\rangle

\displaystyle+\big\langle\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\Rbrack({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f,\partial^{\alpha}f\big\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\delta\|\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f\|_{D}^{2}-C\|\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},{{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}}\Rbrack f\|_{D}^{2}+\big\langle\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\Rbrack({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f,\partial^{\alpha}f\big\rangle.

Here
\Lbrack A,B\Rbrack=AB-BA
is the commutator of
A
and
B
.

Noting that if
\alpha>0
,
\partial^{\alpha}
acts on the local Maxwellian
\mathbf{M}
at least once in the commutators
\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},{{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}}\Rbrack
,
\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\Rbrack
and by the definition of the operator
{{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}}
, we see that there exists a polynomial
\mathcal{U}(\cdot)
with
\mathcal{U}(0)=0
such that

\displaystyle\|\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},{{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}}\Rbrack f\|_{D}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(\|\nabla_{x}[\rho,u,T]\|_{W^{|\alpha|-1,\infty}})\|{{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}}f\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|-1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(\|\nabla_{x}[\rho,u,T]\|_{W^{|\alpha|-1,\infty}})\|f\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|-1}}.

Moreover, by using (
2.1
) and Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality with
\varepsilon>0
, one gets that

\displaystyle\big|\big\langle\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\Rbrack({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f,\partial^{\alpha}f\big\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(\|\nabla_{x}[\rho,u,T]\|_{W^{|\alpha|-1,\infty}})\|({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f\|_{H^{|\alpha|-1}_{D}}\|\partial^{\alpha}f\|_{D}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(\|\nabla_{x}[\rho,u,T]\|_{W^{|\alpha|-1,\infty}})\|({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f\|_{H^{|\alpha|-1}_{D}}\big(\|\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f\|_{D}+\|\partial^{\alpha}{{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}}f\|_{D}\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\bar{\epsilon}\|\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f\|_{D}^{2}+\mathcal{U}(\|\nabla_{x}[\rho,u,T]\|_{W^{|\alpha|-1,\infty}})\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\|({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f\|_{H^{|\alpha|-1}_{D}}^{2}+\varepsilon\|f\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}}\Big).

Putting the above estimates together gives (
2.11
).
The proof of Lemma
2.3
is finished.
∎

Recalling that the nonlinear collision operators
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}
are defined as

\displaystyle\Gamma(f,g)=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{C}(\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}f,\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}g)

and

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f,g)=

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f,\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}g)

respectively, we now state the trilinear estimates on these nonlinear collision operators. The first results is concerned with the trilinear estimates of
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}
without velocity weight.

Lemma 2.4
.

It holds that

\displaystyle\big|\big(\partial^{\alpha}\Gamma(f,g),\partial^{\alpha}h\big)\big|\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}+\tilde{\alpha}=\alpha}\Big(\big|\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}f\big|_{L^{2}}\big|\partial^{\tilde{\alpha}}g\big|_{D}+\big|\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}f\big|_{D}\big|\partial^{\tilde{\alpha}}g\big|_{L^{2}}\Big)\big|\partial^{\alpha}h\big|_{D}

(2.13)

and

\displaystyle\big|\big(\partial^{\alpha}\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f,g),\partial^{\alpha}h\big)\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}+\tilde{\alpha}=\alpha}\Big(\big|\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}f\big|_{L^{2}}\big|\partial^{\tilde{\alpha}}g\big|_{D}+\big|\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}f\big|_{D}\big|\partial^{\tilde{\alpha}}g\big|_{L^{2}}\Big)\big|\partial^{\alpha}h\big|_{D}

(2.14)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}+\tilde{\alpha}+\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha}{\bf 1}_{|\alpha^{\prime}|\geq 1}\mathcal{U}(\|\nabla_{x}[\rho,u,T]\|_{W^{s,\infty}})\Big(\big|\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}f\big|_{L^{2}}\big|\partial^{\tilde{\alpha}}g\big|_{D}+\big|\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}f\big|_{D}\big|\partial^{\tilde{\alpha}}g\big|_{L^{2}}\Big)\big|\partial^{\alpha}h\big|_{D}.

Here and in the sequel,
{\bf 1}_{\Omega}
is the usual indicator function of the set
\Omega
.

Proof.

[
21
, Lemma 3, pp. 406]
with
\theta=0
and
\beta=0
gives (
2.13
). The proof of (
2.4
) is the same as that of (
2.16
) below, we omit the details for brevity. This ends the proof of Lemma
2.4
.
∎

The following lemma is devoted to the trilinear estimates of the nonlinear operator
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}
with velocity weight and velocity derivatives.

Lemma 2.5
.

(i)

For any
\ell\geq 0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\big|\big(\partial^{\alpha}\Gamma(f,g),w^{2}_{|\alpha|}\partial^{\alpha}h\big)\big|

(2.15)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha}\Big(\big|\langle v\rangle^{\ell-|\alpha|}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}f\big|_{L^{2}}|w_{|\alpha|}\partial^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}g|_{D}+\big|\langle v\rangle^{\ell-|\alpha|}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}f\big|_{D}\big|w_{|\alpha|}\partial^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}g|_{L^{2}}\Big)\big|w_{|\alpha|}\partial^{\alpha}h|_{D}.

(ii)

Let
q\in[0,1)
and
\ell\geq 0
,
it holds that

\displaystyle\big|\big(\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f,g),{\langle v\rangle}^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}h\big)\big|

(2.16)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime\prime}=\alpha\atop{\beta^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime\prime}\leq\beta}}\Big(\big|{\langle v\rangle}^{(\ell-|\beta^{\prime}|)}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}f\big|_{L^{2}}\big|{\langle v\rangle}^{(\ell-|\beta^{\prime\prime}|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}g\big|_{D}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\big|{\langle v\rangle}^{(\ell-|\beta^{\prime}|)}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}f\big|_{D}\big|{\langle v\rangle}^{(\ell-|\tilde{\beta}|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\tilde{\alpha}}_{\tilde{\beta}}g\big|_{L^{2}}\Big)\big|{\langle v\rangle}^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}h\big|_{D}

\displaystyle+\sum_{|\alpha^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime\prime}|\leq|\alpha|-m-1\atop{\beta^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime\prime}\leq\beta}}{\bf 1}_{m\geq 0}\mathcal{U}(\|\nabla_{x}[\rho,u,T]\|_{W^{m,\infty}})\Big(\big|{\langle v\rangle}^{(\ell-|\beta^{\prime}|)}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}f\big|_{L^{2}}\big|{\langle v\rangle}^{(\ell-|\beta^{\prime\prime}|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}g\big|_{D}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\big|{\langle v\rangle}^{(\ell-|\beta^{\prime}|)}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}f\big|_{D}\big|{\langle v\rangle}^{(\ell-|\beta^{\prime\prime}|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}g\big|_{L^{2}}\Big)\big|{\langle v\rangle}^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}h\big|_{D}.

Proof.

(
2.15
) directly follows from
[
55
, Lemma 10, pp. 327]
.
We only prove (
2.16
) here. To do this, we first denote
W={\langle v\rangle}^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}

for simplicity, then write

\displaystyle\big|\big(\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f,g),W^{2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}h\big)\big|=\sum C_{\alpha}^{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime}}C_{\beta}^{\beta^{\prime}}G_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}},

where
G_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{6}G_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}}^{i}
with

\displaystyle G_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}}^{1}=-\left(W^{2}\left(\phi^{ij}\ast\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}\left\{\partial^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\left\{\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha^{\prime\prime}}f\right\}\right)\partial_{j}\partial_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}g,\partial_{i}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}h\right),

\displaystyle G_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}}^{2}=\left(W^{2}\left(\phi^{ij}\ast\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}\left\{\partial^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\left\{\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\partial_{j}f\right\}\right)\partial_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}g,\partial_{i}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}h\right),

\displaystyle G_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}}^{3}=-\left(W^{2}\left(\phi^{ij}\ast\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}\left\{\partial^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\left\{\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{v_{i}-u_{i}}{2RT}\right\}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha^{\prime\prime}}f\right\}\right)\partial_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}\partial_{j}g,\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}h\right),

\displaystyle G_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}}^{4}=\left(W^{2}\left(\phi^{ij}\ast\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}\left\{\partial^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\left\{\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{v_{i}-u_{i}}{2RT}\right\}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\partial_{j}f\right\}\right)\partial_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}g,\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}h\right),

\displaystyle G_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}}^{5}=-\left(\partial_{i}W^{2}\left(\phi^{ij}\ast\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}\left\{\partial^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\left\{\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha^{\prime\prime}}f\right\}\right)\partial_{j}\partial_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}g,\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}h\right),

\displaystyle G_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}}^{6}=\left(\partial_{i}W^{2}\left(\phi^{ij}\ast\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}\left\{\partial^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\left\{\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\partial_{j}f\right\}\right)\partial_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}g,\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}h\right).

If
\alpha^{\prime\prime}=0
, since it follows from either (
1.32
) or (
1.33
) that

\rho\sim 1,\ u\sim 0,\ T\sim T_{c},

then performing the same calculation as for proving
[
55
, Lemma 10, pp. 327]
, one sees that all of
G_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}}^{i}
(1\leq i\leq 6)
can be controlled by the first part of the right hand side of (
2.16
).

If
\alpha^{\prime\prime}>0
, using (
1.32
) or (
1.33
) again, we get

\left|\partial^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right|\leq C\mathcal{U}(\|\nabla_{x}[\rho,u,T]\|_{W^{|\alpha^{\prime\prime}|,\infty}})\mathbf{M}^{\frac{3}{8}}

and
